The Committee recommended that the rema ining Ministries for discussion on 
their working may be finalised by consul tation between the Government and the 
Opposition parties at the earliest, failin g which the Chairman may identify the 
Ministries for discussion on their working. 
The Committee, after some deliberations, also recommended that in order to 
ensure the coverage of more Questions during the Question Hour, the Direction by the 
Chairman regarding asking of crisp supple mentaries by Members and precise replies 
by Ministers to Questions/Supplementaries may be strictly adhered to.   
The Committee further recommended that  one supplementary may be allowed 
to the Member in whose name a Starred Qu estion is listed and three other Members 
may be allowed one Supplementary each. This recommendation may, however, be

Demand to reconsider the de cision to allow 100 per cent FDI in the insurance sector 
SHRI SANDOSH KUMAR P (Kerala): Mr. Chairman, Sir, the decision to allow 100 per 
cent FDI in insurance sector was notified by the Commerce Ministry in the month of 
February. So, I take this opportunity to ma ke an earnest demand that this must be 
withdrawn. Sir, there were huge public cries and strikes by trade unions, especially by 
the employees of this sector. But, succu mbing to the pressure of international 
monopolies, Modi Government had to amend the existing provisions and finally it is 
notified. Insurance has a history of legacy of more than 200 years in our country. It is 
not an ordinary business; it is the repository of hard-earned savings of millions of 
Indians. Now, it is under threat. Can this Government ensure that LIC will not be in the 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
17

 
 
 
hands of foreigners in the coming days? Because, नाम जो भी हो, इनका काम surrender 
है। So, they are going to hand over LIC al so. The point is: It is not an ordinary 
business. These people did not create or build up even a single public sector 
undertaking during these ten years, but they  are busy in dismantling all the existing 
structures and all the public sector und ertakings. This needs to be stopped, 
especially in the emerging geo-economic si tuation. We need to protect our economy 
and our insurance sector. Insurance is not only an ordinary business. It is the lifeline of 
this country and it is going to affect hundreds of our workers. Multinational companies 
are coming. Now, they have 70 per cent share in the industry. But, if it is 100 per cent, 
what will be fate of our workers? Who is  going to protect them? These companies 
may start their own subsidiaries or they ma y buy out the existing Indian companies. 
This is going to create a big impact in ou r economy. Sir, through you, I request the 
Government to kindly reconsider this policy of allowing 100 per cent FDI in the 
insurance sector. Thank you.

Shri B. Parthasaradhi Reddy Garu. 
Concern over growing cancer crisis in the country   
SH R I  B .  PAR T H A SA R AD H I  R E D D Y  ( T el an g an a) :  H o n .  C h ai rman  Si r,  I  ri se t o  d raw 
the urgent attention of the Government to the rapidly growing cancer-related issues in 
the country, which is silently becoming a ma jor public health emergency. As may be 
seen, the number of cancer cases in India is  rising sharply every year due to the 
following main reasons. Many cancers are det ected at a very late stage, i.e., Stage III 
and IV. There is lack of routine screening, etc., in rural and semi urban areas of the 
country. There is lack or low level of awar eness about the early symptoms of Cancer-
related problems. Cancer treatment and me dication is very expensive in the country 
and poor and downtrodden people are unable to bear the cost of treatment. Keeping 
in view the rapidly increasing number of pa tients of cancer diseases across India, 
there is an urgent need of more medical, surgical, and Radiation Oncologists. 
Most of the advanced centres are lo cated in cities like Delhi, Mumbai, 
Hyderabad, and cancer- related patients, especially, from  rural parts of India are 
constrained to travel long distances for treatment.  There is a lot of gap in the 
infrastructure. Factors like unawareness of early symptoms of cancer lead to increase 
in the cases of breast, cervical, lung and colorectal cancers. Though the Government 
is making all out efforts to spread awar eness among the people related to cancers 
that occur due to tobacco consumption and environmental pollution, still cancer 
remains a major concern. Millions of families, particularly, the average and middle 
income group people are bein g pushed into financial di stress because of extremely 
high cost of treatment of cancer-related diseases.  
 Sir, I request your kind intervention fo r the Government to take the following 
urgent measures in order to reduce the occurrence of the cancers:  Ensure 
accessibility and affordability of all life-saving  oncology drugs; launch a national mass 
cancer screening mission for early detectio n; design an apt policy to support and 
encourage not-for-profit hospitals by way of providing grant in aids for costly surgical 
and diagnosis equipment. I, therefore, request the Government to treat this issue as a 
national priority and take immediate action in  this regard.  We ought to nip the evil in 
the bud. Sir, I am glad to inform this august  House, through you, that as a part of my 
philanthropic activities, I ha ve established South India’s biggest not-for-profit ultra 
modern 1,500-bedded Cancer Hospital in Hyderabad, namely, Sindhu Hospitals. I 
appeal all the hon. Members of this august House to please visit and utilize such 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
23

 
 
 
facility by referring patients who are und er-privileged irrespective of their socio-
economic background to Sindhu Hospitals.  We are ready to serve them. Thank you 
very much.

Now Shri K.R. Suresh Reddy. 
Demand for ban on  Paraquat Dichloride due to serious health and  
environmental risks  
SHRI K.R. SURESH REDDY (Telangana): Mr . Chairman Sir, this is a request for 
permanently banning the Paraquat Dichlori de because this herbicide is extremely 
poisonous. It is killing our constituents, de stroying rural health and poisoning the soil, 
water and air that our farming communities depend upon. Primarily, this herbicide is 
used for weed control.  This herbicide is easily available in pesticide stores across the 
country, including online platforms such as Amazon and Flipkart. Most of the suicides 
in our country, which are committed, are us ing Paraquat. There are also accidental 
deaths, especially among children who touch this, which is stored in a bottle, or 
consume it and, most of the deaths of child ren are also due to this Paraquat. It is 
reported that around 100 suicide deaths o ccur in Telangana alone, and nearly 1,000 
deaths take place in India every month due to consumption of Paraquat. The 
Telangana High Court has recently directed the State and Central Governments to 
respond to this issue. Mr. Ch airman, Sir, even in Miraj, Maharashtra, reports indicate 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
25

 
 
 
that about 50 deaths occur every month due to Paraquat.  Unfortunately, there is no 
antidote for Paraquat. Normally, such herbicides are banned when they do not have 
an antidote. The emetic solution used in it  should be 0.5 per cent but, in India, the 
product sold contains only 0.1 per cent. So, that does not create a vomiting 
sensation, which would have acted as antidote and have got the pesticide out of the 
system. The Indian Medical Association has recently held meetings across the 
country, including in Hyderabad, and has requested that this herbicide be banned 
immediately. Around 74 countries worldwid e have already withdrawn Paraquat from 
the market, including major markets such as  China, Brazil, the European Union and 
the United Kingdom as well as neighboring countries like Bangladesh and Sri Lanka.  
The Government should order deregistration and ban of Paraquat Dichloride under the 
Insecticides Act of 1968, establish a Para quat Victim Relief Fund to support families 
who have lost members or are suffering long-term health damages. At the same time, 
alternative weed control me thods such as brush cutters, power weeders, and less 
harmful chemicals like glyphosate and other pest-control options should be provided 
on subsidy. This is important because many  farmers use Paraquat extensively without 
being aware of the severe danger it poses.  Therefore, I request the Government to 
immediately respond and impose a national ban on this dangerous pesticide.  Thank 
you, Sir.

Need for greater accountability and lo cal utilisation of  CSR funds by  
private companies 
SHRI PAKA VENKATA SATYANARAYANA (Andhra Pradesh): Thank you, hon. 
Chairman, Sir, for giving me an opportunity. I rise to draw the attention of the august 
House to the urgent need for the greater  accountability and responsibility regarding 
the Corporate Social Responsibility funds. As far as the Companies Act is concerned, 
it has been stipulated that the profits which have been accrued by a particular 
company, at least, 2 per cent of the profits should be spent on the social 
responsibility.  
As the statistics of the Government cl early show, in the Financial Year, 2023-
24, nearly Rs. 34,908 crores have been allocated under this provision. But, actually, 
the talk among the common public is that th e profits were vested with the producers 
and the pollution was left to the public. So , for curtailing the pollution, though so 
many laws have also been ma de, the profits which have b een actually accrued by the 
companies, the producers, they are not utilizing it for cleaning the environment or they 
are not utilizing it for rectif ication of the atmosphere whic h has been polluted by these 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
29

 
 
 
particular companies.  
So, in this regard, I urge the Government, through you, to make a special law 
so that the profits which have been acq uired by those companies may be spent 
exclusively for rectification of the mist akes which have been committed by these 
industries, by way of cleanin g the air pollution or other things. Though the Pollution 
Control Boards are there and a number of laws  are in place, they are submitting their 
reports to the concerned judicial system admi tting that public is suffering. But, at the 
same time, they are indicating that CSR fund s are non-lapsable and assuring that the 
problem would be rectified in the forthc oming years. In this manner, they are 
postponing almost every issue. So, I urge, through you, to the Government, that the 
monies or funds which have been accrued by the companies should be exclusively 
utilised for a clean pollution so that the correct corporate social responsibility is 
discharged in this regard. Thank you, Sir, you for giving me this opportunity.

Need for comprehensive judicial reforms to ensure speedy justice  
SHRI P. WILSON (Tamil Nadu): Thank you, Mr. Chairman, Sir, for giving me this 
opportunity. Sir, the regional benches of Supreme Court are very important to be 
established as they are access to the justice. In the last five years, out of 593 Judges 
appointed to the High Court, 473 Judges, th at is, nearly 80 per cent belongs to the 
forward communities. Even in the Supreme Court today, there is only one woman 
Judge, two Judges belonging to religious minority, one Judge from the Scheduled 
Caste, and there is no Judge from Schedu led Tribe. There is a need for a broader 
social representation so that the judiciary re flects the diversity of our society. Thirdly, 
the strength of Judges in our higher ju diciary is grossly inadequate. The Judge 
population ratio in our country remains low. The High courts have numerous 
vacancies while even the sancti oned strength is not sufficient. Sir, we must legislate 
to offer a mechanism to suspend Judges f acing serious allegations with clinching 
evidence without compromising the independence of judiciary to retain public faith in 
the judiciary. Impeachment is a long-drawn and extremely complicated process.

SHRI SUKHENDU SEKHAR RAY: Sir, the ancien t twin cities of Gaur and Pandua in 
West Bengal served as the capital of Pala and Sena empires of Bengal for more than 
400 years. The place has been mentioned even in our old scriptures. The 
Archaeological Survey of India maintain s 21 monuments, archaeological sites and 
remains which are situated in Malda district of West Bengal. In the 1930s, the British 
Government made an aerial survey in the Pandua or Pandunagar area and decided for 
vigorous excavation, but it has not materializ ed so far for one reason or the other.

year old fortified city of Devikot, mentio ned in our ancient scriptures. It was first 
excavated by the Calcutta University in 1938.  Now, recently, excavation was resumed 
by the Government, but for some unknown reasons, it has been stopped. Similar 
situation is with Chandraketugarh, anot her ancient site of Bengal, which has 
relevance with the history of Mohenjo Daro and Harappa. Sir, my first supplementary…

MR. CHAIRMAN: Q. No. 212. 
Social security for unorganised workers 
*212. SHRI SANDOSH KUMAR P : Will the Minister of LABOUR AND EMPLOYMENT 
be pleased to state: 
(a)  the number of unorganised workers in the country; 
(b)  the number of unorganised workers protected under social security schemes; 
(c) the reasons for huge number of non-protected workers in the unorganised 
sector; and 
(d) whether the Ministry has any plan to  initiative any specific programme to 
provide socialsecurity for all the workers in unorganised sector? 
THE MINISTER OF LABOUR AND EMPLOYMENT (DR. MANSUKH  
MANDAVIYA): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) to (d) As per economic survey 2021-22, the total work force estimated in 
the informal sector is about 43.99 crore.   
46
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
  India has achieved a hist oric milestone in the realm of social protection 
coverage, recording one of the most signifi cant expansions globally. According to the 
latest data from the International Labour Organization’s (ILO) ILOSTAT database, 
India’s social security coverage has incr eased from 19% in 2015 to 64.3% in 2025. 
India’s expansion in social protection co verage has been enabled by a strong 
foundation of digital and financial inclusion. 
  In order to extend social security be nefits to the workers in the unorganised 
sector, the Central Governme nt has launched the eShram  Portal on 26.08.2021 for 
creation of a National Database of Unorganised Workers (NDUW) seeded with 
Aadhaar. As on date, more than 31 crore unorganised workers have registered on a 
self-declaration basis in the eShram portal, which facilitates access to various social 
security and welfare schemes. 
  Unorganised workers are also being covered under various social security 
schemes implemented by the Government. Major schemes include the following:  
(i)    Pradhan Mantri Shram Yogi Maandhan (PM-SYM)  
(ii)  National Pension Scheme for Traders and Self-Employed Persons (NPS-
Traders) 
(iii)   Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana (PMJJBY) 
(iv)   Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY) 
(v)    Atal Pension Y oiana (APY) 
(vi)    Pradhan Mantri Kisan Maandhan Yojana (PM-KMY)  
(vii)   Ayushman Bharat-Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB PM-JAY)  
  The details of the abov e schemes are enclosed at Annexure-I. 
  Through these schemes and programmes implemented by different 
Ministries/Departments, workers in the un organised sector are provided pension 
security, life and accident insurance, and health protection. 
  The Code on Social Security, 2020 has come into force on 21.11.2025 with the 
objective to consolidate and simplify the exis ting social security laws into a single 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
47

 
 
 
framework and to extend social security cove rage to all workers, including organized, 
unorganized, gig, platform, and self-employe d workers, by ensuring universal access 
to social security benefits, promoting po rtability of benefits across employments and 
States, enabling the Central and State Gove rnments to frame appropriate schemes, 
leveraging technology for efficient deliv ery, and clearly defining the roles of 
governments, employers, and aggregators in  order to provide comprehensive social 
protection to the workforce in line with evolving labour market conditions.  
 In keeping with the Budget Announ cement 2024–25 to develop eShram as a 
One-Stop Solution for unorganised work ers to access various social security 
schemes, the Ministry launched the eShram “One-Stop-Solution” on 21 October 
2024. This initiative entails integration of di fferent social security and welfare schemes 
on a single portal, i.e., eShram, enabling registered workers to access schemes and 
view benefits availed by them through the portal. 
 So far, fourteen (14) schemes of diffe rent Central Ministries/Departments have 
been integrated with eShram to extend benefits and access to social security, 
insurance and skill development programmes to eShram cardholders across the 
country. These include Pradhan Mantri Street Vendors Atmanirbhar Nidhi (PM 
SVANidhi), Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY), Pradhan Mantri Jeevan 
Jyoti Bima Yojana (PMJJBY), Viksit Bharat – Guarantee for Rozgar and Ajeevika 
Mission (Gramin) (VB-GRAMG), Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana – Gramin (PMAY-
G), Ayushman Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY), Pradhan 
Mantri Awas Yojana – Ur ban (PMAY-U), Pradhan Mant ri MatsyaSampada Yojana 
(PMMSY), Pradhan Mantri Kisan Samman Nidhi (PM-KISAN), One Nation One 
Ration Card (ONORC) and Pradhan Mantri Matru Vandana Yojana (PMMVY).  
 Apart from the above, eShram has linka ges with the National Career Service 
(NCS) for job opportunities, Skill India Di gital Hub (SIDH) for skill development, 
Pradhan Mantri Shram Yogi Maandhan (PM-SYM) for pension benefits and the 
convergence portal of the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA). The 
details of the number of unorganized work ers registered on eShram portal and 
enrolled under various social welfare schemes is enclosed at Annexure-II.

Annexure-I 
Sl. No. Details of Schemes Enrolments 
1.  Pradhan Mantri Shram Yogi Maandhan (PM-SYM): A 
voluntary and contributory pension scheme for 
unorganised workers aged 18-40 years with monthly 
income up to Rs. 15,000 Th e subscriber contributes 
between Rs. 55 and Rs. 200 per month, depending 
on the age of entry, and the Government provides an 
equal matching contribution. The subscriber 
receives a minimum assured pension of Rs. 3,000 
per month after attaining the age of 60 years. 
Approx 52.47 lakhs 
(as on 28.02.2026) 
2.  National Pension Scheme for Traders and Self-
Employed Persons  (NPS-Traders):  A pension 
scheme for shopkeepers, traders and self-employed 
persons with annual turnover not exceeding Rs. 1.5 
crore and aged 18-40 ye ars, providing minimum 
assured pension of Rs, 3,000 per month after the 
age of 60 years, with matching contribution by the 
Government. 
Approx 61 thousand 
(as on 28.02.2026) 
3.  Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana 
(PMJJBY): A life insurance scheme providing life 
cover of Rs. 2 lakh for death due to any cause at a 
nominal annual premium of Rs. 436. 
Approx 26.79 crore 
(as on 18.02.2026) 
4.  Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY): An 
accident insurance scheme providing Rs. 2 lakh 
coverage for accidental death or permanent disability 
and Rs. 1 lakh for partial disability at an annual 
premium of Rs. 20. 
Approx 57 crore 
(as on 18.02.2026) 
5.  Atal Pension Yojana (APY): Atal Pension Yojana was 
launched on 9th May, 2015, with the objective of 
creating a universal social security system for all 
Indians, especially the poor, the under- privileged 
and the workers in the unorganised sector. Itis open 
to all citizens of India between 18-40 years of age 
Approx 8.84 cr. 
(as on 28.02.2026) 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
49

 
 
 
having a savings bank account in a bank or post-
office. For better targeting of guaranteed pension to 
unorganised sector workers, an income tax payer 
shall not be eligible to join APY from 1st October, 
2022. The subscriber under APY is required to make 
a monthly/quarterly/six monthly contribution of an 
amount determined by the amount of pension 
chosen and the age of joining the scheme. The 
subscriber shall receive a government guaranteed 
minimum pension of Rs 1000 per month, Rs. 2000 
per month, Rs. 3000 per month, Rs. 4000 per month 
or Rs. 5000 per month, after the age of 60 years until 
death, depending on the contribution chosen.  
6.  Pradhan Mantri Kisan Maandhan Yojana (PM-KMY):  
A voluntary contributory pension scheme for small 
and marginal farmers aged  18-40 years, providing 
minimum assured pension of Rs. 3,000 per month 
after attaining the age of 60 years, with matching 
contribution by the Government. 
 
Approx 19.92 lakh 
(as on 09.03.2026) 
7.  Ayushman Bharat - Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya 
Yojana (AB PM-JAY):  A flagship health assurance 
scheme providing health cover of Rs. 5 lakh per 
family per year for secondary and tertiary 
hospitalization to economica lly vulnerable families, 
including a large number of workers from the 
unorganised sector. 
Approx 43.60 crore 
(as on 09.03.2026)

MR. CHAIRMAN: Q.No.213.   
National Mission on Libraries 
#*213. DR. DINESH SHARMA: Will the Minister of CULTURE be pleased to state: 
(a) the status of implementation of Nation al Mission on Librar ies, including the 
number of libraries upgraded and digitized during the last three years, 
State-wise; 
(b) the status of modernization of public library infrastructure, along with the 
details of major reforms undertaken  to expand digital access through 
national platforms and for capacity building of library professionals;  
                   
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
55

 
 
 
(c) the details of improvements made in  public accessibility, utilization and 
preservation of knowledge resources, State-wise; and  
(d) whether the impact of reforms under  the Mission has been assessed in 
terms of accessibility, utility and long-term sustainability and if so, the details 
thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF CULTURE AND TOURISM (SHRI GAJENDRA SINGH 
SHEKHAWAT): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) to (d) The Ministry of Culture through National Mission on Libraries (NML) 
Scheme, under its ‘Setting up of NML Model Library’ component, provides financial 
assistance to one State Central Library and one District Library in each State /UT on 
the recommendation of the Stat e Authorities concerned and 6 Libraries identified by 
Ministry of Culture. The financial assistan ce is provided for i) Up-gradation of 
Infrastructure, ii) Modernization of Tec hnology & Modernization of Services, iii) 
Creation of Facilities for Specially abled us ers and iv) Subscription of E-journals/E-
books for selected Libraries under NML, v) Network Connectivity for District Libraries, 
vi) Procurement of Reading resources an d Advocacy & Outreach programs etc. The 
details of financial assistance provided to State /UT under NML during the last three 
years are given at Annexure-A. 
The Indian Culture Portal (ICP) provides a unified digital platform offering 
access to diverse cultural and knowledg e resources from across the country by 
enabling online access to curated and original  content. It promotes and showcases 
both the tangible and intangible cultural heritage of India through various categories 
which include manuscripts, museum artifacts,  paintings, archival photographs, food 
and culture, musical instruments of India,  archival documents, social practices, 
rituals, and festivals, Oral traditions and expressions and rare books, etc. These 
details are provided for every region of India and from Indus Valley Civilization to the 
present.  
The Capacity Building Programmes (CBP ) are also organized under the NML 
across the Country to provide training to lib rary professionals of all States/UTs. Till 
date 54 number of CBP have been organi zed and 2465 library professional have been 
trained. 
56
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
An evaluation of NML has been carried out by Quality Council of India and the 
Report was submitted in November, 2025. As per QCI Report, the National Mission on 
Libraries remains a significant intervention  for India’s transition into a knowledge-
based society. It has contributed to digita l access, infrastructure  upgrades, and staff 
capacity building.

Restoration of monuments 
*214. DR. ASHOK KUMAR MI TTAL: Will the Minister of CULTURE be pleased to 
state. 
(a) the reasons why ASI-managed mo numents are deteriorating due to 
delayed restoration work;  
(b) whether staff shortages or insufficient funding have been identified as 
major causes and if so, the details thereof and  if not, what other reasons 
have been identified therefor; 
(c) the reasons why preventive conservation is not being implemented 
despite repeated audit observations; and 
(d) whether the Ministry is addressing misuse of funds meant for structural 
repairs and preservation, and if so, the details thereof and if not, the 
reasons therefor? 
THE MINISTER OF CULTURE AND TOURISM (SHRI GAJENDRA SINGH
SHEKHAWAT): (a) to (d) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) There are 3686 centrally protecte d monuments/sites under the care 
and maintenance of Archaeological Su rvey of India (ASI) and there is 
no monument/site in deteriorating co ndition due to delayed restoration 
work.  
(b) ASI takes up conservation work of all centrally protected 
monuments/sites as per the need of  the monuments and availability of 
resources. There is no staff shortage and insufficient funding for 
conservation of centrally protected monuments.  
(c) Preventive conservation is regula rly implemented by the field offices. 
Daily maintenance, general upk eep of monuments, removal of 
vegetation, etc is regularly carried out to keep the monument 
presentable and in a good state of preservation.  
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
63

 
 
 
(d) ASI takes up conservation work as per the approved ACP/Estimate duly 
approved by the Competent Authority and expenditure is incurred as per 
the approved ACP/Estimate. Guidelines have been issued to field offices 
to follow General Financial Rules, Archaeological Works Code, CVC 
guidelines and any other codal formalities and to maintain transparency.

SHRI SANT BALBIR SINGH (Punjab): & Sir, the development of villages depends on 
basic infrastructure—roads, water manage ment, drinking water supply, housing 
schemes, and employment programmes lik e MGNREGA. For all these development 
activities, adequate financial resources ar e essential. Unfortunately, funds amounting 
to more than ₹8,000 crore meant for Punjab have been stopped. Without these funds, 
rural development activities are severely affected. Through you, Sir, I request the hon. 
Minister to kindly ensure that these pend ing funds are released to Punjab. Under the 
Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana, about ₹828.87 crore was sanctioned for roads 
and bridges in Punjab. Under this scheme, 64 roads and 38 bridges were proposed to 
be constructed. However, the tenders for these projects were linked to FDR 
technology, and in the last six to seven months, no company has come forward to bid 
using that technology. As a result, all these works have come to a standstill. In many 
villages, these 64 roads are still incomp lete or have not been taken up for 
construction. I request, through you, Sir, that the sanctioned amount of ₹828 crore 
should be allowed to be utilised so that these roads can be built. Roads are the lifeline 
of rural areas. Farmers, labourers, and v illagers depend on them  for transportation, 
livelihood, and access to services. When road construction stops, the overall 
development of villages also comes to a halt. 
I would also like to bring another issue to the attention of the Government. 
Farmers in Punjab are facing serious difficulties as no one is willing to purchase the 
potatoes produced in the State. The market price has fallen below ₹200 per quintal, 
and heaps of potatoes can be seen lying on  the roads. I request the Government to 
intervene and provide support to farmers by ensuring procurement or appropriate 
market support, so that they are encouraged to sow the next crop. 
Sir, the development of villages depends on ensuring employment for rural 
labourers and providing farmers with pr oper infrastructure. When funds under 
MGNREGA and other rural development sche mes are not released, villagers suffer 
greatly. Many development works such as road construction, water supply systems, 
and housing scheme require funds from th e Rural Development Department. If the 
State Government does not receive its due funds, it cannot contribute its share for 
these schemes, and the projects remain incomplete. At present, the biggest 
challenge we face is the blockage of th e Rural Development Fund. I request the hon. 
Minister, through you, Sir, to kindly consider the plight of the people. What is the fault

of the villagers that they should suffer because funds are withheld? People are waiting 
for the completion of pending works and are asking when the funds will come and

and labourers are hardworking people who co ntribute immensely to the nation. They 
actively participate in and support every Government scheme through their hard work. 
I, therefore, urge the Government to release the pending funds at the earliest. 
Punjab is also a border State and th e development of its rural areas has 
strategic as well as economic importance. I once again request that the funds meant 
for rural development be released without delay so that the pending works can be 
completed and the villagers do not continue to suffer. Thank you, Sir.

SHRIMATI RAJATHI (Tamil Nadu): & Hon. Deputy Chairman Sir, thank you for giving 
me this opportunity to speak.  Mahatma Gandhiji once said, “India’s soul lives in its 
villages.” I myself come from a remote village, and I have also served as a Panchayat 
Union leader for five years. Through that experience, I have understood the real needs 
of rural areas. These needs include basic amenities such as proper roads, drinking 
water, medical care, electricity, and employment opportunities. However, today, the 
ruling Government seems to believe that mere ly enacting laws from Delhi or allocating 
funds on paper will automatically fulfill all the needs of the villages. But what are the 
real needs of a village? What are the real n eeds of a State? Only the people of that 
village and the respective State Governme nt can truly understand and decide these 
matters. Under this Government, the prices  of fertilizers and seeds have increased 
drastically, severely affecting the livelihood o f  f a r m e r s .  W h a t  k i n d  o f  l a w s  h a s  t h i s  
Government enacted for their welfare? In fact, the most significant action of this 
Government, in my view, was the introduction of the four controversial farm laws that 
caused great distress among farmers across th e country.  But when we look at Tamil 
Nadu, the situation has been different. During the rule of the Dravida Munnetra 
Kazhagam in 1974, under the leadership of Dr. Kalaignar Karunanidhi, the then Chief 
Minister, every village in the State was fu lly electrified. He was also the one who 
introduced 100 percent free electricity for farmers. Even today, under the leadership of 
our hon. Chief Minister, Thiru M.K. Stalin, free electricity connections have been 
provided to 2.5 lakh farmers. In the present situation, people in rural areas expect only 
basic facilities - good roads, safe dr inking water, proper medical care and 
employment opportunities. But what is the thinking of the hon. Prime Minister and this 
Government today? It appears that they believe governance can be carried out only 
                   
& English translation of the original speech delivered in Tamil. 
112
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
through announcements and advertisements. The people of this country know that 
such illusions cannot address the real problems. 
Today, the Central Government has not released ₹4,034 crore to Tamil Nadu 
under the MNREGA scheme. Similarly, ₹3,458 crore allocated for education has also 
not yet been released. The Government itse lf admitted on February 6 that each State 
is supposed to receive ₹1,200 crore. With elections approaching, our hon. Chief 
Minister announced that the rightful funds for Tamil Nadu would surely be received. 
But unfortunately, our voices have not been heard and the funds have still not reached 
us. 
Sir, I am also a member of the Stan ding Committee on Rural Development. 
During our study tours to various States, officials themselves informed us that 
sufficient funds have not been released. I, personally, witnessed this situation in West 
Bengal and Tamil Nadu. However, when we visited Madhya Pradesh, we were told 
that funds had been fully received for their projects. This raises a serious question in 
my mind: why is the Government adopting such a discriminatory approach? We also 
live in this country. Our States are an integral part of India. A united India must include 
and respect all its States equally. At the sa me time, allocations for major rural welfare 
schemes have been drastically reduced. Th e allocation for the MNREGA scheme has 
been reduced from ₹86,000 crore to ₹30,000 crore. The allocation for the Pradhan 
Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana has been reduced from ₹19,000 crore to ₹11,000 crore. 
Similarly, the allocation for the Pradhan Ma ntri Awas Yojana has been reduced from 
₹54,832 crore to ₹32,500 crore.  Hon’ble Deputy Chairman Sir, I would like to ask: is 
there any reason or justification for reduci ng these crucial alloca tions? For effective 
governance, the Central Government must function cooperatively with State 
Governments and listen to their concerns  and opinions. Only when we build a 
cooperative and harmonious relationship wi th the States, can the development of the 
nation be complete. Arbitrary decisions ta ken without consultation will not help the 
country progress. 
I would also like to highlight another im portant issue. Recently, I read news 
reports about Bihar and Uttar Pradesh where many children died due to malnutrition, 
largely because of the lack of basic me dical facilities. Studies published in 
newspapers last week revealed these disturbi ng facts. In many villages, there are still 
no adequate medical facilities, proper road s, or reliable electricity supply. If a 
Government fails to provide these basic nece ssities, it is difficult to understand the 
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
113

 
 
 
reason for its pride. I, therefore, humbly re quest that greater attention be given to the 
development of rural infrastructure. Rural people are not greedy like urban 
populations. Their expectations are simple  and basic—they only ask for fundamental 
amenities necessary for a dignified life. 
Finally, I would say, you may attempt to hide the name of Mahatma Gandhiji or 
even move his statue from one place to another. But his greatness can never be 
hidden. The memories and ideals of Mahatma Gandhiji can never be erased from the 
hearts of the people.  With these words, I conclude my speech. Thank you.

Need for revival of large card amom cultivation in Sikkim  
SHRI DORJEE TSHERING LEPCHA (Sikkim): Sir, I draw attention of the Government 
towards the need for revival and strengthening of large cardamom cultivation in the 
State of Sikkim, which has long been known as the traditional home of this valuable 
spice. Large cardamom is not only an impo rtant cash crop but also a major source of 
livelihood for thousands of small and marginal farmers in the Himalayan region. 
In recent years, the production of la rge cardamom in Sikkim has declined 
significantly due to several challenges, su ch as plant diseases, climate variability, 
aging plantations and lack of adequate scientific support. This decline has directly 
affected the income and economic security of farmers, who depend heavily on this 
crop.  
In this regard, the role of the Spices Board India becomes extremely important. 
The Board has been implementing various schemes for the development of 
cardamom cultivation, including replantation, quality planting material distribution and 
technical training for farmers. However, th ere is a need to further strengthen these 
interventions specifically for Sikkim, considering the unique agro-climatic conditions 
of the State.  
[ 12 March, 2026 ]
127

 
 
 
I urge the Government of India to e nhance financial and technical support 
through the Spices Board for large-scale rejuvenation of cardamom plantations, 
establishment of disease-resistant nurseries , research on climate-resilient varieties 
and improved post-harvest processing fac ilities. Special incentives may also be 
provided to encourage young farmers to co ntinue cardamom cultivation. Revival of 
large cardamom cultivation will not only boos t farmers’ income but also strengthen 
India’s position in the global spice market.

Demand for establishment of  AIIMS hospitals in Samb alpur, Koraput and    
Baleshwar in Odisha  
SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha): Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir, there is a critical need 
for the establishment of AIIM S hospitals in Sambalpur, Koraput and Baleshwar in the 
State of Odisha.  Sir, Odisha, with its diverse geography, requires comprehensive 
healthcare facilities spread across its regi ons to ensure equitable access to quality 
medical care. Currently, we have an AIIMS in Bhubaneswar, which has significantly 
improved healthcare standards in the region . However, the western region of Odisha, 
represented by Sambalpur, and the coastal region, represented by Baleshwar, along 
with the tribal-dominated Koraput, still l ack such advanced medical infrastructure. 
The establishment of AIIMS hospitals in these locations will address the healthcare 
disparities and bring high-quality medical se rvices closer to the people. These AIIMS 
hospitals should not merely be satellite facilit ies but full-fledged institutions like AIIMS 
Bhubaneswar, with all necessary departme nts, advanced medical equipments and 
specialized staff. This will ensure that residents of these regions receive the same level 
of care as those in Bhubaneswar.   
By setting up AIIMS hospitals in Sambal pur, Koraput, and Baleshwar, we will 
not only improve healthcare accessibility,  but also boost medical education and 
research in these areas. This initiative w ill be a significant step towards creating a 
healthier and more equitable Odisha. Thank you.

Need to enhance allo cation under MPLADS  
SHRI MASTHAN RAO YADAV BEEDHA  (Andhra Pradesh):  Sir, I seek to raise an 
important issue concerning the Members of Parliament Local Area Development 
Scheme (MPLADS), which is a vital instru ment for addressing local developmental 
needs. At present, each Member of Parliament is allocated Rs. 5 crore per year under 
the MPLADS. However, the effective valu e of development works executed on the 
ground is considerably reduced due to stat utory deductions such as GST at 18 per 
cent, seigniorage charges of around 5 pe r cent, quality control charges and other 
mandatory recoveries during implementation. As a result, the actual developmental 
output comes down to nearly 75 per cent of the sanctioned amount. Consequently, 
from the nominal allocation of Rs. 5 crore, the real value of works executed is only 
about Rs. 3.5 to Rs. 3.75 crore. This limi ts the ability of MPs to adequately meet 
growing local demands relating to rural in frastructure, drinking water, education, 
health facilities and community assets. At the same time, the cost of construction 
materials, labour and infrastructure deve lopment has increased substantially due to 
130
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
 
inflation and rising input prices. Despite these changes, the MPLADS allocation has 
remained unchanged for several years, maki ng it increasingly difficult to undertake 
meaningful and impactful projects, particularly in rural and backward areas. In view of 
these realities, I urge the Government to consider enhancing the annual MPLADS 
allocation from Rs. 5 crore to at least Rs. 10 crore per Member of Parliament. Such an 
increase will enable MPs to respond more  effectively to constituency needs, 
strengthen grassroots infrastructure and ensure better developmental outcomes for 
the people. Thank you, Sir.

Need to address the iss ues of linkage between academia and industry  
SHRI RYAGA KRISHNAIAH (Andhra Pradesh): Sir, premier academic institutions and 
industries tend to forge ahead in different directions as both streams have their own 
vested interests. However, the Governments have long ago realized the importance of 
industry-academia symbiosis for mutual benefit. As State funding to the universities is 
on the decline, such collaborations become the need of the day for self-reliance and 
optimization of resources. But profit speci ficity and confidentiality issues of the 
industries stand in the way of a health y commensalism. Industries can provide 
financial support for projects, boosti ng academic research capacities. Human 
resources of the universities and funding support by the industries can be lethal 
combination. For example, Boeing’s grants to universities for aerospace research 
have driven advancements in aviation tec hnology. Universities can develop curricula 
integrating practical, industry-relevant skills. Th is is particularly relevant to bridge the 
employability gap. Universities could identi fy areas of cooperation with the local 
industries, to begin with. Ultimately most  of the graduates from the universities 
gravitate towards the industries and indu strial compatibility is a prime necessity.

Concern over the incident of adulterated milk in Rajamahendravaram,   
Andhra Pradesh 
SHRI SUBHAS CHANDRA BOSE PILLI (Andhra Pradesh): In late February 2026, the 
residents in localities such as Swar oop Nagar and Chowdeswari Nagar in 
Rajahmundry, Andhra Pradesh,  fell ill with symptoms like vomiting, abdominal pain 
and acute kidney function (failure to produce urine) after consuming milk supplied by 
local dairy vendor. Samples of milk and c linical specimens were sent to labs for 
forensic and chemical analysis.  Many pe ople died after consuming the suspected 
milk with death toll rising to 8 to 9 as pe r the official updates. 20-25 persons were 
hospitalized who are threatened with kidn ey complications requiring dialysis and 
ventilator support and are in critical condition. The Food Safety and Standards 
Authority of India (FSSAI) sought a detailed report and is monitoring the case closely 
with Andhra Pradesh State Government. The contaminated milk was traced back to a 
local diary unit in Narasapuram Village, Ko rukonda Mandal, East Godavari District. 
The Government and officials have issued a detailed public Health Bulletin on the 
incident, confirming that many people we re diagnosed with symptoms like severe 
kidney stress, high blood urea and high cr eatinine levels and these are the symptoms 
of kidney failure. I urge the Central Gove rnment to order a detailed enquiry in the 
matter. I also urge the Government to pr ovide financial assistance to the affected 
families. Thank you, Sir.
